The scene now has its own settings (`StarportSettings.lighting`: no baked or realtime lightmaps, environment lighting only, as the Spaceflight scene has) and its baked lighting data (`Starport/LightingData.asset` and two reflection probe textures), so nothing is baked on load. Pictures of the hall before and after are the same: the average colors of the hull, floor, wall and sky are identical in every scheme tried, and the few pixels that differ are where the astronaut's idle pose and the hull's anti-aliased edges vary between runs. The new probe scenario `starport-lighting` fails both of its checks on the old code (no lighting data or settings; the message printed twice) and passes both on the new.
- **Armor Class Equivalency**: armor bought in Ship Configuration always came with the full points of its class, whatever damage the ship had, and buying it did nothing for the hull. It now works as the original's does (CONFIG-OV): the plating adds 250 points a class to the points the bare hull has, so unrepaired damage stays, and the ship counts the class those points are worth, the class bought times the share of its full points the ship has, rounded down. Class 3 armor bought onto a hull down to 150 points gives 900 points and counts as class 2, and class 1 armor on any damaged hull counts as no class at all. When the class is lower the panel shows the original's warning, "Warning: armor class equivalency will be lower due to unrepaired damage". Buying armor also makes the hull's integrity whole again, as the original sets the armor's condition to 100, and that condition is the hull's. The points a purchase can leave over a lowered class (at most 249 over the class's full points) are kept when a game is loaded, and the Starport repair sets the armor to its class's full points, as the original's repair does. The new probe scenario `starport-armorequivalency` passes only 1 of its 8 checks on the old code (the control: armor on a whole hull) and all 8 on the new, and `starport-savedata`'s check of old saves now expects armor points to be cut back to that cap rather than to the class's full points.
- **Unanalysed Artifacts Show "???"**: the Trade Depot's Sell and Analysis lists showed what every artifact in the hold is worth, so there was nothing to learn from an analysis. They now show "???" for the value of an artifact of a kind that has not been analysed, as the original's do (IT-OV, TDEPOT-OV), and its value once it has been. The Buy list still shows the Starport's prices, and a sale still pays an artifact's value whether it was analysed or not, also as the original's. The new probe scenario `starport-unknownartifacts` passes only 2 of its 4 checks on the old code (the Buy list's control and the value after an analysis) and all 4 on the new.
- **Specimens Sell for Half Their Value**: the Trade Depot paid the full value saved with a captured specimen and listed it at that value. The original's Sell screen pays a specimen's value times its share for sales, 50% (TDEPOT-OV), and lists it at the same, so a specimen now sells for half the value saved with it, and the Sell list shows what the sale pays (`PD_Specimen.GetSaleValue`). The value saved at capture is unchanged. The probe scenario `starport-specimens` now checks the listed value and the payment (617 for a specimen worth 1,234): the old code passes only its first check (the specimen is listed), and the new code passes all three.
- **Endurium Price Fixed at Launch**: the price of Endurium followed the date wherever the ship was, so a rise on 20-02-4620 or 15-05-4620 changed what a ship already in flight paid and was paid. The original sets the price only when the ship leaves the Starport (PORTMENU-OV), so a rise during a flight counts from the next launch on, also for the towing fine, which reads the same price. The price of the day is now kept when the crew goes aboard (`PD_Starport.m_enduriumPrice`, saved) and used until the next launch. Before the first launch, and in a save from before the price was kept, the price of the day is used as before. The new probe scenario `starport-enduriumlaunch` launches on day 40 and trades on days 60 and 140: the old code passes only its setup and its control for older saves (2 of 5 checks), and the new code passes all 5.
- **Trade Depot Keeps Ten Curios**: every artifact sold to the Trade Depot stayed in its stock for good, so the Buy list grew with every sale. The original's Trade Depot keeps the ten curios it bought last and throws the others away each time the ship leaves the Starport (TDEPOT-OV, run at launch by PORTMENU-OV), and never throws away its special artifacts, the 15 with an analysis of their own (the original's ARTIFACT records 1 to 15: the Crystal Orb to the Black Box). The port now does the same when the crew goes aboard (`PD_Starport.LimitArtifactStock`, `GD_Artifact.IsSpecial`). The new probe scenario `starport-artifactstock` sells a Rod Device and twelve curios and launches: the old code keeps all twelve curios and passes only its setup and its two controls (3 of 4 checks), and the new code passes all 4.
- **Colonies Rated by the Original's Rules**: a planet recommended for colonization earned a bonus only if it was on STRINFO's list of 51 suitable planets, at the listed amount, and the reasons for an unsuitable one came from the port's reading of the planet tables. The original rates the planet itself as the captain logs it (CAPTAIN, OP-OV). Six checks make it unsuitable, in this order: extreme heat (the whole temperature range above tropical), extreme cold (all of it below temperate), crushing gravity (over 2.00 G), violent storms, lack of oxygen (no nitrogen-oxygen or oxygen-carbon dioxide air, and no oxygen sea) and lack of water (no water, and no oxygen-hydrogen air). A planet that passes them earns 5,000 MU a point: 1 for each end of its temperature range a colony can stand, 2 for 0.70 to 1.30 G (1 for any other), 2 for calm or moderate weather, 1 for life, and its mineral density over a fifth of the scale (at least 1). It is optimal above 40,000 MU. The port now does the same (`GD_Planet.GetUnsuitableReasons`, `GetColonyBonus`). Applied to every planet, the rules give 50 of the list's 51 evaluations exactly. One of the list's planets is a slip for its neighbour in the same system, which scores the listed 35,000 while the printed one fails four checks. They also pay five Tropical-Inferno planets the list does not have. The new probe scenario `colonyrating` books every planet: the old code passes only its control, the 50 listed planets (1 of 4 checks), and the new code passes all 4.
- **Four Artifacts Known at the Start**: a new game knew two artifacts without an analysis, the Whining Orb and the Flat Device, so the Red Herring and the Terrain Vehicle Debris showed "???" on the Trade Depot's Sell list. In the shipped game's new-game data (`START\STARB.COM`) all four have their analysis byte set, so the game data now marks the Red Herring and the Terrain Vehicle Debris known at the start too. The new probe scenario `starport-knownartifacts` fails both of its checks on the old data and passes both on the new.
- **A Dead Crew Member's Posts Go to the Best of the Others**: when a crew member died, only the captain's post moved, down the chain of command of the 1984 notes; every other post stayed with the dead member, so a dead engineer stopped all repairs, a dead navigator aimed with no skill, and a dead doctor treated nobody. The original does more (HEAL-OV): a dead member's skills are lost, each post they held after the captain's goes to whichever crew member holding a post has the best skill for it (the captain if nobody has any), and a dead captain's command goes to the science officer. The port now does the same (`PD_CrewAssignment.ReassignPostsOfTheDead`, run on every death). The new probe scenario `crewposts` kills the navigator, then the engineer and then the captain: the old code passes only the captain's control (1 of 3 checks), and the new code passes all 3.
- **The Crew Heals as in the Original**: only the doctor's patient healed, at 2 points of vitality a second for a doctor of medicine skill 100, and nobody else ever recovered. The original heals in ticks (HEAL-OV), one every (260 - the doctor's medicine) quarters of a second, from 1.25 s with the best doctor to 65 s with none; each tick gives every living crew member on board 1 point and the patient 2 (1 on a planet's surface), and the doctor says "<name> is healed." when the patient reaches 100. The port now does the same (`PD_CrewAssignment.UpdateTreatment`, `GetHealTickSeconds`), so healing is much slower than before and nobody aboard stays hurt for good; with a dead doctor the post passes to someone else, so the treatment no longer stops for want of a doctor. The probe scenario `m20` now checks the ticks: the old code passes only its first treatment check and the check of the dead (7 of 11 checks in all), and the new code passes all 11.
- **Alien Ships' Readings Fall with Their Damage**: the sensors read an alien ship's Bio and Energy as its vessel's 100 or 0, however badly the ship was hit. The original reads what is left of the ship's armor and shields over its vessel's, at most 100 (SCIENCE), for a ship that carries life or has power, and a damaged ship with life gives 75 to 85% of that as its energy. The port now does the same, so a scan shows how close a ship is to breaking up. The new probe scenario `alienreadings` passes only its control on the old code (1 of 2 checks: a whole ship), and both on the new.
- **AI Sandbox Runs the CLI in a Private Folder**: the optional AI orchestration (`Assets/Scripts/AI`, Editor only) started `claude -p` in one fixed folder, `Application.temporaryCachePath/ClaudeCli`, and reused it if it was already there. On Linux that path is under the shared `/tmp`, so another user of the machine could make the folder first and put project settings with hooks in it, which the CLI would then run as the developer; the system prompt file also had a predictable name and was written through any file or link already at that path. Every call now makes a new folder with a random name in the user's temp folder (`ClaudeCliChatService.CreateRunDirectory`), writes the prompt there as a new file only (`FileMode.CreateNew`), passes `--setting-sources user` so that no project or local settings are read from it, and removes the folder afterwards. Windows and macOS were not exposed, since their temp folders are per user. The assembly compiles with the Semantic Kernel define, and a run against a stand-in CLI gave the same prompt, input and replies as before.
- **Notices Give Time to Read Them**: Operations typed a notice out a paragraph every 0.3 s, so the 15 paragraphs of the first notice were all on the screen in about 4 s and its first orders had scrolled away before they could be read. The original pauses after each paragraph for 300 ms a byte of the paragraph's encoded text (OP-OV), from 5.7 s for a short line to 42 s for the longest, and the spacebar shows the next paragraph at once. The game data now holds each paragraph's length in the shipped game (`GD_Notice.m_paragraphLengths`, read from the new-game file), and the panel pauses for it (More still skips ahead). The probe scenario `starport-noticetyping` now checks the pauses after the first two paragraphs of the first notice, 40.5 and 34.5 s: the old code fails that check (3 of 4), and the new code passes all 4.
- **Report to Operations After a Tow or a Lost Terrain Vehicle**: a ship towed back to the Starport, or back without its terrain vehicle, could launch again at once from its docking bay, and its crew could use every room of the Starport before Interstel's evaluation and its fine were booked. The original sends the crew to Operations first (SHIPBUTTONS, PORTMENU-OV, OP-OV): the captain's Launch in the docking bay says "Report to Operations for evaluation." and the ship stays, disembarking says the same, Personnel, Crew Assignment, the Bank, Ship Configuration and the Trade Depot beep and stay shut, and Operations brings up the evaluation by itself a second after it opens; once it has been shown, everything opens again. The port now does the same (`PD_General.MustReportToOperations`, `AstronautController.EnterDoor`). The new probe scenarios `launchreport` and `starport-reportrooms` pass only their controls on the old code (1 of 2 and 1 of 3 checks), and all their checks on the new.
- **An Analysis Uses Up Its Sensor Reading**: the science officer analyzed a planet's reading once ("I have already attempted analysis of this data, Captain.", from the 1984 notes), but an alien ship's reading could be analyzed again and again, each time with a new roll of the dice, until every line came out certain. In the original every analysis uses up the reading it was made of (ANALYZE-OV), whatever was read, and the next one says "I need a current sensor reading." until the sensors read again. The port now does the same (`SensorsDisplay.m_analyzedReadingCount`, which also replaces a static field that outlived the scene). The probe scenario `analysisskill` now checks both: the old code fails them (3 of 5 checks), and the new code passes all 5. The `AnalyzeScan` helper and the `cargo` scenario, which analyzed one reading several times, now take a new reading for each analysis.
- **A Planet's Analysis Says "Not Certain"**: a line of a planet's analysis the science officer could not tell read "Unknown", and the orbit number could be unknown too. The original prints "NOT CERTAIN" for such a line (ANALYZE-OV ?.CERTAIN), as the port already did for an alien ship's lines, and always gives the orbit number (.ORBIT is never in doubt). The port now does the same. The probe scenario `analysisskill` checks that a science officer of no skill gives the object's class and the orbit number with the other five lines "Not certain": the old code fails that and the count of uncertain lines at skill 100 (3 of 5 checks), and the new code passes all 5.
- **The Crew Left at the Starport Rests**: a crew member who stayed at the Starport while the ship was away never recovered, so an injured officer taken off the crew stayed injured for good. The original rests them at every launch (PORTMENU-OV): each living crew member on file who is not on board gains 20 points of vitality for each day since the ship last left, up to 100. The port now does the same when the crew goes aboard (`PlayerData.RestCrewAtTheStarport`), keeping the day of the last launch in the save (`PD_General.m_lastLaunchDay`; a save from before it rests the crew from day 0 on its first launch). The new probe scenario `starport-rest` passes only its control on the old code (1 of 3 checks: the crew on board and the dead are unchanged), and all 3 on the new.
- **Probe's Memory Meter Works with Burst Off**: since the probe started Unity with Burst off (#406), `starport-transport` failed its control every time: the check that the memory meter sees a 256-byte array taken 20,000 times. In the Starport scene a garbage collection then ran during each of the meter's five tries at those 5 MB, so the meter reported "could not be measured", shown as 0 bytes a call. The control now takes 2,000 arrays (512 KB), which the heap's few-kilobyte blocks still resolve to a byte or two a call (it measures 292 bytes a call, the array and its header). The game is unchanged. `starport-transport` failed its control 2 of 2 times on master and passes 2 of 2 with the change; with Burst on it passed either way. `perframe` and `leaks`, which use the same meter, still pass.
- **Merge Rule Matches the Branch Protection**: the repository's working rules still said `master` had no branch protection and that GitHub's auto-merge was not to be used. Since 2026-10-16 `master` requires the `compile` check, so the rules now say a pull request is merged by arming squash auto-merge, which waits for that check to pass, never with `--admin`. Documentation only.

## [1.1.0] - 2026-10-15

Combat as the original's. The player's ship now takes hits the way the original's does: they fall on the shields, then the armor, and only then on the hull, the systems and the crew, and the ship is lost when its hull is, not its armor (#389). The weapons do the original's damage, about five times what they did, falling off with distance, with alien lasers twice as strong and the Uhlek plasma bolt a missile that follows the ship (#390). The navigator's skill decides whether a shot hits, and every shot burns the original's fuel (#391). The Starport's hall, the station and the docking bay now take the colors of the chosen scheme (#382, #388), and the documentation and this changelog were rewritten in one voice. On this release's code the headless probe passes all of its scenarios, and the automated playthrough plays a new game through to the ending and Interstel's bonus.

### Added

- **Contributing Guide**: `CONTRIBUTING.md` explains how the project is run: the fidelity principle, one change per pull request, conventional commits, the changelog, the code style and how changes are tested.
- **Starport Follows the Color Scheme**: the Starport's hall (the 3D concourse the astronaut walks through) and the station the ship launches from kept the classic navy and cyan in every color scheme. They now take the colors of the scheme in use, in its dark mode, as befits a 3D view: the hull, the wall, the doors and the transporter pad take the theme roles they had in the classic kit (the panel navy, the border, the secondary text, the sensor window, the dim accent with a soft glow, the second accent with its glow), and they change the moment another scheme is picked (`StarportColours` sets a property block per renderer and material, so the material assets keep the classic dark theme's colors that the scenes are saved in). The floor used to be a texture baked in the classic colors; its albedo map now holds the weights of five theme colors (the floor, the glowing band around the star, the star, the door mats and the dark area behind them), which `SF - Standard` blends in a new palette mode (`SF_PALETTEMAP_ON`, `SF_PaletteColor0` to `4`), so a single texture draws the floor in any scheme. In the schemes of the original games (Starflight and Starflight 2) the station wears the 1986 Starport's own EGA colors instead (`Research/Screenshots/Starport`): a light cyan floor with a light blue star, a blue wall, red doors, a grey hull and pad, and a glowing brown edge on the pad. `UIKitConcourseTool` now writes the weights (`Starport Floor - Kit Weights.png`, linear, high quality compression) in place of the colored texture, which is removed, and gives the materials the classic dark theme's colors whatever scheme the Editor has selected; the table of which color goes where lives in `StarportColours` and is shared by both. In the classic scheme the hall looks as before (before and after pictures differ only on the floor, by a few levels at most, on the anti-aliased edges of the band and the door mats). The new probe scenarios `starport-hallcolors` (every scheme, the 1986 colors in the original games' schemes, a light mode, the assets left untouched, and with `-Graphics` a picture of the hall in each scheme) and `stationcolors` (the station in the Spaceflight scene) pass only their controls on the old code (2 of 6 and 1 of 2) and all of their checks on the new.
- **Docking Bay Follows the Color Scheme**: the docking bay (the hangar the ship waits in before launch) kept the classic kit's blue grey and amber in every color scheme. It now takes the colors of the scheme in use, in its dark mode, as the Starport does (`StarportColours`, whose table now holds the bay's materials too): the walls a blend of the theme's two text colors, the siren bases its border color, the doors their palette (`Docking Bay Doors - Kit Weights.png` holds the lightness of the metal and of Interstel's logo, which `SF - Standard`'s palette mode blends with the theme's metal and logo colors; the colored door texture is removed), and the sirens, both their domes and their lights, the theme's second accent. The launch and landing animations used to set the sirens' glow in the classic amber; they now set only how brightly the sirens are lit, in grey, and a new emissive tint supplies the color (`SF_EMISSIVECOLORTINT_ON`, `SF_EmissiveColorTint`), set in the renderer's own property block so that the animated value still shows. The sirens' eight lights still switch on and off with the animations (brightness 0 before the launch, 1.25 during it), now in the scheme's color. In the Starflight and Starflight 2 schemes the bay wears its colors from before the new look: white walls, grey doors with a yellow logo that does not glow, and orange sirens on grey bases. `UIKitDockingBayTool` writes the door weights and the grey siren keys, and gives the materials the classic dark theme's colors whatever scheme the Editor has selected. In the classic scheme the bay looks as before (before and after pictures differ only on the anti-aliased edges of the logo and the door lettering, by at most 66 levels on 0.15% of the pixels). The new probe scenario `dockingbaycolors` passes only its three controls on the old code (3 of 7: the four materials found, the sirens dark before the launch, the assets left untouched) and all seven on the new.

### Changed

- **Comments Record Decisions, Not Who Made Them**: code comments, tool docstrings, probe notes and recovered data notes that credited a design decision to a person now simply give the decision and its date ("decided 2026-10-11", "chosen 2026-10-12"), and the ship model tools' comments describe the textured models rather than the tool they came from. Comments and notes only: no code, file or asset name changes, and nothing in the game behaves differently.
- **Documentation Rewritten**: the README, the roadmap, the research notes and the tool READMEs were rewritten for clarity and in one consistent voice. The README now opens with a proper introduction and gains a section on the port's ships, aliens and lifeforms, the roadmap carries a spoiler warning, and decisions in every document are recorded as decisions with their dates. The code review of 2026-10-03 is reworded and keeps every open finding; the two superseded project notes (`PROJECT_CONTEXT.md`, `PROJECT_ANALYSIS_REPORT.md`) are removed. The ship model README is rewritten around the textured model pipeline. Documentation only: no code, file or asset names change.
- **Changelog Reworded**: every entry of this changelog was reworded for clarity and in one consistent voice, keeping each change's details, file names, PR numbers and test results. A few entries that only described local development tooling were folded out. Documentation only.
- **The Hull Decides When the Ship Is Lost**: hits on the player's ship now land the way the original's do (DAMAGE-OV). A hit is split into chunks, each a random share of what is left and at least 10 points, and each chunk lands on one thing: the shields while they are raised and charged (outside a nebula), otherwise the armor while it has points, and once the armor is gone one of the ship's working parts chosen at random (the hull, a system or the crew), which loses 10 times the chunk divided by the hull's integrity. Better shields and armor lose less of each chunk ((6 - class) / 5 of it), and shields no longer pass on to the armor what they cannot hold. The ship is destroyed when its hull's integrity reaches 0 ("Hull destroyed!"), not when its armor runs out: a ship with no armor left fights on and can still be saved, and its systems and crew are no longer damaged while the armor holds. The engineer repairs the hull and the systems but never the armor, which only the Starport restores, and the Starport charges 250 M.U. per point of hull integrity. The status display's damage reading now reflects the hull and the systems rather than the armor (their average health, critical once the hull is at 19 or below). Older saves load with an intact hull, and armor below 0 in an old save becomes 0. The new probe scenario `hullintegrity` covers all of this (each of its nine checks fails on the old code), and the scenarios that relied on the old rules now destroy the ship through its hull (`DestroyWithAHit`). This is the first of three steps toward the original's combat; weapon damage is unchanged for now.
- **Weapons Do the Original's Damage**: lasers and missiles now deal the original's damage (DAMAGE-OV), about five times what they did before: a laser does 90, 210, 360, 510 or 660 by class, a missile 200, 400, 700, 1000 or 1500, and the Uhlek plasma bolt 4000 (it did 100). The plasma bolt is now a missile that follows the ship, as in the original, so a ship that stays away until it burns out escapes it; it used to strike at once, like a laser. A laser's damage falls off with distance, by a tenth for every cell of the original's combat map, down to nothing at 10 cells; a cell is 80 units in the port, so the laser's 800-unit range matches the original's 10 cells. Alien lasers do twice a laser's damage (a quarter of that with the Rod Device aboard), and aliens now open fire only within 5 cells of the ship in each direction (400 units, down from 1000), so their lasers always do at least half damage. Together with the hull integrity change, fights are now as quick and as deadly as the original's. The new probe scenario `weapondamage` covers the damage, the falloff, the firing reach and end-to-end hits (each of its five checks fails on the old code).
- **The Navigator Aims, and Every Shot Burns Fuel**: the player's shots now follow the original's aim (COMBAT-OV). Each axis of a shot is off by whole cells of the combat map unless a roll under 250 minus the navigator's skill comes in below 50, so a shot lands on target with a chance of (50 / (250 - skill)) squared: one in 25 for an untrained navigator, a quarter at skill 150, and every shot from skill 200 up. A laser that is off target misses, and a missile that is off target flies to the spot it was aimed at and goes off there. Until now the navigator's skill did nothing. A laser shot burns a tenth of a cubic meter of Endurium and a missile half a cubic meter, as in the original (they burned 0.01 and 0.02), and the missile's half is taken all at once. Shield recharge during a fight stays as the port has it: the original adds 20 points per turn of its combat loop, and how long a turn lasted depended on the 1986 computer. The new probe scenario `aimandfuel` covers the aim, its odds and the fuel end to end (each of its five checks fails on the old code); the probe's crew has a navigator of skill 250, so the scenarios that fire to hit still hit, and H8 and the combat scenario now count the new fuel per shot.

### Fixed

- **Playthrough Test Fights On After Loading a Save**: stage 12 fights the Uhlek of encounter 261 and saves in the middle of the fight, and stage 13 loads that save, fires on them and flies out. Since alien ships fire their laser and launch their missile independently (#372), stage 12's 55 seconds stripped all 1,500 shield points and left the hull at 575 of 1,000, and stage 13 then failed one way or another: either an Uhlek scout's class 5 missile (200 points) landed before the first weapon press and the fight loop immediately called the fight lost ("0 presses of the weapons", seen on master 58f728fc and again from the same save), or, given time to fire, three missiles in 9 seconds destroyed the ship on the way out. Stage 12 now ends the first part of the fight with the shields at half, stage 13 breaks off once its weapons have hit and the shields are gone, and the fight loop (`PtFight`, which takes the condition at which a stage has had enough) never breaks off before the weapons have been tried (an alien hit, or three presses) unless the hull is down to a quarter. Test tooling only. Verified: from the same stage 11 save, `run-playthrough.ps1 -From 12` passed stages 12 to 21 in three of three runs (42 checks each), and from the stage 12 save of the last run, `-From 13` passed stages 13 to 21 in three of three runs (38 checks each); stage 12 saved with 715 to 730 shield points and the hull intact, and stage 13 broke off with the hull at 960 or more and finished with 760 to 775.

## [1.0.0] - 2026-10-14

The first complete release, and the milestone this port has been building toward: *Starflight* is playable from a new game all the way to its ending, in a Windows build (`Starflight-1.0.0-win64.zip` on the GitHub release page). Every item on the roadmap to parity with the original is done or decided (59 of 59), the game rules follow the original's decompiled code wherever it is known, and every screen has been compared side by side with the original. On the release code the headless probe passes every one of its scenarios (more than 200, with over 1,100 checks), and its automated playthrough plays a new game through to the ending and Interstel's bonus.

### Added

- **Player's Manual and a Player-Facing README**: `docs/MANUAL.md` explains the game without spoilers: the backstory, the controls, the Starport's rooms, every console button, flight, planets, aliens, combat, money, the ways the game can end and the known differences from the original, with tables of crew races and prices. The README now opens with what players need: screenshots (`docs/images/`, rendered by the probe's screen picture scenarios), how to download, unzip and run the Windows build, what to do when Windows blocks an unsigned program, the controls, the 1.0.0 status and the credits. The developer sections follow; the feature list, which names the game's secrets, sits behind a spoiler warning, and two of its lines are corrected: the Rod Device cuts alien lasers to a quarter (#374), and the Starport also repairs damaged systems (#368).
- **Full Playthrough Test**: the probe scenarios `playthrough-1` to `playthrough-21` play the whole game headless, from a new game to the win and the 500,000 MU bonus, each stage loading the previous stage's save and using the real console buttons with real flight and driving (`DevTools/HeadlessProbe/run-playthrough.ps1`, about 25 minutes). It uncovered the bugs fixed in #339, #340, #342, #343, #345 and #348 and the boarding distance and Black Egg countdown bugs, and passes all 122 of its checks with those fixes.
- **Livelier Starfield**: within each layer of the spaceflight starfield (`InfiniteStarfield`, eight layers wrapping around the camera for parallax), every star had the same size and colour, drawn from 8 by 8 pixel sprites that looked blocky up close. Each star now has its own size and brightness (many faint, few bright, as in a real sky) and a colour from blue white to orange, tinted by its layer; the brightest 3.5% get a short cross-shaped glint, and nearly half twinkle gently, each at its own pace. A dedicated shader (`Assets/Resources/Shaders/Starfield Star.shader`) draws a sharp core and a soft halo with no texture, computing the twinkle on the GPU from each particle's stable random values, so it costs no CPU time per frame. The new properties come from a separate random sequence; the starfield still takes exactly four of the game's random numbers per star, so later draws are unchanged. The `starfieldpictures` probe scenario checks sizes, colours, glints and twinkle (all four fail on the old code) and that the game's random numbers are drawn as before.
- **Fifteen Color Schemes, Selectable at Any Time**: the UI kit had a single look in two modes, navy and steel grey. It now ships fifteen schemes, each with dark and light modes, switchable at any time: the save game panel (Escape) has a Colors button in place of Light Mode, opening a screen that shows every scheme with seven swatches (screen, panels, text, the two accents, fuel and danger) and Dark and Light buttons. The whole game switches instantly, and the choice is stored in the computer's settings (`PlayerPrefs` "UI Kit Scheme", by name, beside "UI Kit Mode"). The schemes are Classic (the kit as it was, the default); Starflight, the 1986 original's sixteen EGA colours as seen in `Research/Screenshots` (white values and light cyan labels on black in blue frames, the green selected button, brown headings, the magenta sensor window); Starflight 2, the 1989 sequel's sixteen EGA colours as captured from a GOG copy of the game (a light grey screen around black panels in blue frames, dark cyan labels with light blue values, the green selection bar, red shield and yellow armor gauges, the magenta sensor window); and twelve palettes shortlisted from the palette study of 2026-10-13 (Endurium, Interstel Amber, Phosphor, Titanium, Heritage EGA, Solar Gold, Spec-Ops, Azure Drive, Arctic Glass, Starliner, Night Ops and Hi-Vis). Each scheme's two themes are assets in `Resources/UI Kit Themes/`, generated by `DevTools/UIKitSchemes/make_themes.py`: `palettes_spec.py` holds the key colours, and `derive.py` derives the 24 colour roles in OKLCH and lifts every text colour to WCAG AA contrast, while the two original games' schemes keep their own colours. `UIKitTheme` gains `c_schemes`, `GetScheme`, `SetScheme` and `LoadScheme`. `UIKitModeApplier` maps from the current theme to the next, renders text colour tags in the theme's colours in every theme except classic dark, and still recognises objects created after their scene changed theme by the colours the scenes are saved in. Console bullets are drawn from each theme's colours at run time (`UIKitTheme.GetBullet`, sharing the drawing with `UIKitBuilder`), so classic light mode's bullets are now its own teal. The 3D views keep their colours. The `colorschemes` probe scenario checks all fifteen schemes in both modes (all six checks fail on the old code), `lightmode` switches the mode on the Colors screen (its two checks of that fail on the old code), and `PROBE_UI_SCHEME=<name>` runs any scenario in a given scheme.
- **Starmap Follows the Color Scheme**: the starmap was purple and magenta whatever the UI kit looked like (its background, grid and ship marker are flat-coloured materials, and the ship's log shares the background). Since the starmap is a view of space, its background, grid, ship marker and crosshair now take the active scheme's dark mode colours: the panel colour behind, the dim accent for the grid, the accent for the ship and the text colour for the crosshair. They update immediately when another scheme is picked. Stars and nebulas keep their colours, and the materials are untouched (`Starmap.UseSchemeColours`: property blocks on the starmap's renderers, plus a copy of the background material for the two images drawn with it). Verified with the new `starmapcolors` probe scenario: its two colour checks fail without the change, and all three pass with it.
- **View Overlays Stay Dark in Light Mode**: in every light mode, labels drawn over space used the mode's dark accents and text, which barely showed against black space (the STAR SYSTEM tag in the view's corner, the target bracket, star and planet labels, the countdown), and the ship's log was dark text on its dark background, still the old purple until the starmap had been opened. Anything drawn over a 3D view now uses the active scheme's dark theme in either mode (`UIKitTheme.GetViewTheme`): `UIKitStyle` and `UIKitModeApplier` handle everything in the main viewport (under the object with `Viewport`, texts' colour tags included), the target bracket, the terrain labels with their scan colours and the starmap request it, and the ship's log colours its own background when it opens (`Starmap.UseSchemeBackground`). The console and the panels beside the view keep the light mode. The terrain scan's lifeform count, written in a pink of its own that no scheme changed, now uses the kit's alert colour. Verified with the new `viewoverlays` probe scenario: five of its six checks fail on the old code, and the sixth, a control, passes on both.
- **Light Mode for the UI Kit**: the kit had one look, navy and near black. It now has a steel grey light mode (`Resources/UI Kit Theme Light`: every area in the dark steel grey of its borders, #7A8591, the selected row a shade lighter and panel lines a shade darker, with near black text and accents for the most contrast this grey allows: 5.3:1 for main text, about 4:1 for accents), and the save game panel gains a Light Mode / Dark Mode button, built at run time from its Resume button. The choice is stored in the computer's settings (`PlayerPrefs` "UI Kit Mode"), not in a saved game. `UIKitTheme.SetMode` switches the theme and raises `UIKitTheme.Changed`: every `UIKitStyle` reapplies itself, the message box and console redraw, and `UIKitModeApplier` recolours the rest (button tints, the colours the kit's tools copied into the scenes, including the scene that is never unloaded, and in light mode the colour tags in texts, via a TextMesh Pro preprocessor). Dark mode is unchanged, and the 3D views keep their own colours. The `lightmode` probe scenario checks the switch both ways (all three checks fail on the old code), and `PROBE_UI_MODE=light` runs any scenario in light mode.
- **Spemin Comm Portrait Comes to Life**: on the comm screen the Spemin only breathed in depth and glanced around. It now moves like the Genesis version's portrait (the research notes' source for animated comm screens): `SpeminCommAnimation` on the existing hand-made Spemin model blinks its eyes, spins its discs on their stalks (each with its own speed and direction), wobbles the body in width and height as well as breathing, and has slime swell on the ceiling, stretch, fall and splash onto the goo floor. It finds the parts by name, creates the drips when the portrait starts and uses its own random generator, leaving `Random.state` alone; it takes over `SpeminBreathe`'s breathing, while the eyes and stalks keep their `RandomRotation`. The `commspemin` probe scenario checks all four motions over 8 s of a settled link (the old portrait fails all four) and with `-Graphics` captures 12 frames. This is the pilot for the other races' comm portraits.
- **Elowan Comm Portrait Comes to Life**: on the comm screen the Elowan's vines only glanced around now and then (`RandomRotation` on some bones; the other bones, the stalk and the leaves never moved), and its eyes never blinked. Following the Spemin, it now moves like the Genesis version's portrait: `ElowanCommAnimation` on the existing hand-made, rigged Elowan model runs a curl along each vine from stalk to tip with a gentle twist at another pace, sways the two stalk bones below the head, turns each leaf about its midrib at its own pace, and now and then blinks both eyes, squashing each along its upward axis and easing in and out. It finds the parts by name, moves each from its pose when the portrait started, takes over the vines' `RandomRotation` (the head keeps its glances) and uses its own random generator, leaving `Random.state` alone. The new `commelowan` probe scenario checks that every vine bone, both stalk bones and every leaf move and that the eyes blink (the old portrait fails all four), and that no frame jumps at a steady 30 frames per second; with `-Graphics` it captures 12 frames.
- **Sci-Fi Buttons**: the player's choices were flat rows of text (the console menu) and flat tinted panels (the buttons of the Starport rooms, the save panel and the questions). They are now the kit's sci-fi button: a fill with its top left and bottom right corners cut off, tinted as before, and a thin frame in the accent colour with a short tick in each cut corner (`UI Kit - Button`, `UI Kit - Button Frame`, the theme's `Button` and `ButtonFrame` sprite roles). `UIKitModeApplier.MakeButton` gives every kit-tinted button this look when its scene loads (the frame is a child with its own `UIKitStyle`, so it follows each mode's colours). The console puts a button behind each row, the highlighted one lit: centred on the middle of the row's capitals, as tall as the row spacing minus a gap, the text 14 pixels from the left edge, and anchored in proportions of the console's height like its labels, so it stays with its row at any screen size. The bar beside the selected row is gone, since the frame marks it. The `scifibuttons` probe scenario checks the console, the save panel and the light mode's frame colour (all three fail on the old code).
- **Compile Check CI**: `.github/workflows/compile-check.yml` runs a headless compile with the project's Unity version on every pull request and push to `master` via GameCI (`game-ci/unity-test-runner`, EditMode). It needs the `UNITY_EMAIL` and `UNITY_PASSWORD` repository secrets, plus `UNITY_SERIAL` (Pro/Student) or `UNITY_LICENSE` (Personal). A manual run (`workflow_dispatch`) accepts an optional `unityVersion` to compile against another editor. A final step lists any committed `Packages/` or `ProjectSettings/` files Unity rewrote during the run.
- **Unity CLI Editor Bridge**: `com.unity.pipeline` 0.8.0-exp.1 (experimental) lets the Unity CLI drive an Editor with the project open: `unity status` to find it, then `unity command console`, `recompile`, `editor_play`, `eval` and about 160 more, so the console and compile results can be read from the command line. Its server listens on 127.0.0.1 only and needs a per-Editor token. It is compiled out of release builds and stays off in development builds unless enabled in its settings. It brings back `com.unity.nuget.mono-cecil` as a dependency.
- **Headless Play-Mode Probe**: `DevTools/HeadlessProbe/` runs the real Spaceflight or Starport scene headless in play mode, with an in-memory save system so no save file is touched, and checks game behaviour through scenarios, one for every fix made since 2026-10-03. A scenario starts once the first star system's planets are generated, since the game is paused until then. `probe.ps1` runs one scenario, `run-all-scenarios.ps1` runs them all, and `compile-check.ps1` is the local compile check. The folder sits outside `Assets/`, so it is not part of the build, and `.gitignore` keeps the copy a run puts into `Assets/` from being committed.
- **Encounters in Orbit**: the game data has twelve encounters waiting in orbit around a planet (home fleets, the Veloxi drones, a derelict), and nothing ever started them. Entering orbit around the planet now begins the encounter: the ship drops out of orbit into it, the aliens arrive from the planet's side, and when the player flies out the ship is back at star system level, where it was before entering orbit. This is the original design (`Research/Notes/gameflow.txt`). Entering orbit again restarts it for as long as it has living ships, and after a launch from the surface it waits for the launch animation to end. The planet is not drawn in the encounter yet. **Data**: four of the twelve name an orbit with no planet in the game data (encounters 139, 165, 302 and 305), so those four still cannot begin; the game data was not changed.
- **Planet Beneath Orbital Encounters**: an encounter begun in orbit was fought in empty space, though the ship had just entered orbit around a planet. As the 1984 notes describe (`gameflow.txt`: "If an encounter occurs when a ship is in orbit the ship drops to a tactical map which has a planet in it ... Ships and weapons move OVER the planet, and it scrolls around and does not affect anything"), and as ruling 5 left for a later step, the planet now lies beneath the encounter: the orbit view's sphere with the planet's own surface, sized by its mass, below the ships and centred under the ship's position when the encounter began (`Encounter.ShowPlanetBackdrop`). The notes' planet was a disc in three sizes and six colours; the port draws the planet itself, at a size and depth of its own choosing. Verified with the `orbitplanet` probe scenario: a Spemin encounter met in hyperspace has no planet (the control), and the derelict in orbit 3 of star 30 is fought over its planet's own surface; the check fails on the previous code. With `probe.ps1 -Graphics` it also renders the encounter into `Logs/orbit-encounter-pictures/`.
- **Ancient Ruins Placed by STRINFO's Editors**: STRINFO's notes list three ancient ruins that hold only Endurium. One stands on the Mechans' "ice planet in our system", with 3.1 cubic meters (2.1.4). Two stand together where the Thrynn say "a fairly large ancient ruin" lies, with 3.1 and 5.9 cubic meters (2.5.4). The port had the alien lines pointing to them but not the ruins. They are now records in `Starflight Recovered Data.json` (`m_ancientRuinSiteList`, `GD_AncientRuinSite`), resolved with the other recovered records. `TerrainRuins` places them without random numbers, each holding its Endurium as a random ancient ruin does (taken once). The Red Cylinder detects them, and their planets get no random ruins ("nothing else of note is there"). STRINFO puts both Thrynn ruins at one spot; the port puts the second 40 units east of the first. Found in the audit of the recovered texts against STRINFO (roadmap Phase 7). Verified with the `ancientsites` probe scenario: both checks fail before the change and pass after.
- **Crew Reports When Aliens Raise Shields or Arm Weapons**: in the original, the crew says "CAPTAIN, THEY'VE RAISED THEIR SHIELDS.", "...LOWERED THEIR SHIELDS.", "...ARMED THEIR WEAPONS." and "...DIS-ARMED THEIR WEAPONS." whenever the aliens' shields or weapons change during an encounter (COMM-OV, `<AUX>` with RAISE-SHIELDS, LOWER-SHIELDS, ARM-WEAPONS and DISARM-WEAPONS in the decompiled original, s-macke/starflight-reverse). The port now does too, from the state the Analysis reads (`Encounter.UpdateShieldsAndWeaponsNews`, after `AliensHaveShieldsUp` and `AliensAreArmed`): the starting state is taken silently, as the original's IAPWR sets it, and nothing is said once no alien ship is left. Found in the screen comparison (roadmap Phase 7). Verified with the `alienshields` probe scenario: its three checks of the lines fail before the change and pass after, and its control (nothing said at the start) passes on both.
- **Sensor Pictures Traced From the Original**: `DevTools/SensorPictures/` builds a vessel's sensor picture (the 1024 x 512 background and mask of `SensorsDisplay`) from a screenshot of the original game's sensor window in `Research/Screenshots/Ships/`. It extracts the ship from the magenta window (coloured parts go into the mask, where the scan's colour noise shows), traces its outline into polygons that straighten the original's pixel staircases, and draws them centred at the scale of the existing Spemin pictures (with the original's 1.2 pixel aspect). `make.ps1` writes both textures with their `.meta` files, assigns them to the vessel's slots in `Spaceflight.unity` and writes a review sheet next to the original. The first picture is the Elowan transport (vessel 5), which used to leave the sensor window empty; the other vessels without a picture follow once this one has been reviewed. Verified with the `sensorpictures` probe scenario: the new check that an Elowan transport scan shows its own picture fails on the old scene and passes after.
- **Tool for Reading the Original Game's Records**: `DevTools/OriginalRecords/walk_records.py` walks the record tree in the original game's `STARB.COM` as the decompiled original's extractor does, and prints the field bytes that the decompiled `instance.txt` omits. Its `--races` table gives each race's conversation values (COMM-OV): the starting "emotional disposition level", its three thresholds and the effects of the player's postures, the shipped values for what the port's mood model (roadmap 2.1) had picked numbers for. Tooling only; no game change.
- **Sensor Pictures of the Elowan Scout and Warship**: the other two Elowan ships (vessels 6 and 7) get sensor pictures traced from screenshots of the original by `DevTools/SensorPictures`, at tolerance 0.6 like the transport (the Elowan are an organic race), with review sheets in `DevTools/SensorPictures/previews/`. The `sensorpictures` probe scenario now checks every traced picture from a list that grows with each race (both new entries fail on the old scene), and its checks of a scan with no picture use an unknown object, since every vessel will have a picture. Verified with that scenario: all checks pass.
- **Sensor Pictures of the Thrynn Ships**: the Thrynn transport, scout and warship (vessels 8 to 10) get sensor pictures traced from screenshots of the original by `DevTools/SensorPictures`, at tolerance 0.6 (an organic race), with review sheets. The warship's sprite fills most of the original sensor window, so its picture is drawn at two thirds of the usual scale to fit inside the port's. Verified with the `sensorpictures` probe scenario: the three new entries fail on the old scene, and every check passes after.
- **Sensor Pictures of the Veloxi Ships**: the Veloxi transport, scout and warship (vessels 11 to 13) get sensor pictures traced from screenshots of the original by `DevTools/SensorPictures`, at tolerance 0.4 (machines, so single pixel steps stay steps), with review sheets. Their screenshots are larger than the others (1066 x 800), and the tool converts them to the 320 x 200 screen; the transport and warship fill most of the original sensor window and are drawn at about two thirds of the usual scale. Verified with the `sensorpictures` probe scenario: the three new entries fail on the old scene, and every check passes after.
- **Sensor Pictures of the Gazurtoid Ships**: the Gazurtoid scout and warship (vessels 14 and 15) get sensor pictures traced from screenshots of the original by `DevTools/SensorPictures`, at tolerance 0.6 (an organic race), with review sheets. Their spiky outlines come out in several separate pieces (4 and 10 outlines), as in the original sprites, and the colour noise falls on their seven and eight coloured lights. Verified with the `sensorpictures` probe scenario: the two new entries fail on the old scene, and every check passes after.
- **Sensor Pictures of the Uhlek Ships**: the Uhlek scout and warship (vessels 16 and 17) get sensor pictures traced from screenshots of the original by `DevTools/SensorPictures`, at tolerance 0.4 (single pixel steps stay steps), with review sheets. Their crescent hulls fill most of the original sensor window, so they are drawn at about three quarters of the usual scale. Verified with the `sensorpictures` probe scenario: the two new entries fail on the old scene, and every check passes after.
- **Sensor Pictures of the Enterprise and the Noah 9 Derelict**: the last two vessels without a sensor picture, the Enterprise (vessel 21) and the Noah 9 derelict (vessel 23), get pictures traced from screenshots of the original by `DevTools/SensorPictures`, at tolerance 0.4, with review sheets. The derelict has no coloured pixels in the original, so its picture has no colour noise. Every vessel in the game data now has a sensor picture; an unknown object still leaves the window empty. Verified with the `sensorpictures` probe scenario: the two new entries fail on the old scene, and every check passes after.
- **Notices Screen Matches the Original**: the Operations notices screen said "Today is" with the date, ended a notice with nothing, and with no new notices showed the last one again without asking. The decompiled original (OP-OV: WE735, WE912, WEA28) heads the screen with NOTICES and today's date, writes "***END OF NOTICES***" after the newest notice, and with nothing new says "NO NEW NOTICES" and asks "REPEAT LAST NOTICE? [N Y]", repeating it after "***REPEATING LAST NOTICE***" on Y. `OperationsPanel` now matches: the date beside the scene's Notices heading, the end line, and the question, which the screen's More and Quit answer as Yes and No (relabelled while it is asked); No returns to the menu. The original's line-by-line typing of a notice with an abort is left out (section 18 of the screen comparison, `Research/Screen comparison.md`, keeps it open). Verified with the new `starport-notices` probe scenario (4 of its 5 checks fail on the old code, the fifth being the control; all pass on the new); `starport-savedata` checks the new header, and `starport-win`, `starport-input` and `shipslog` pass.
- **Repair Lets the Player Choose**: the engineer repaired the worst item first and, once it was whole, moved on to the next by himself (a design decision of 2026-10-11, made when no shipped source showed how Repair worked in the original). The decompiled original does show it (ENGINEER, `(/REPAIR)`): Repair puts a cursor on the DAMAGE display, the stick moves it through the seven rows, and the text window describes the row under it ("ENGINES: SLIGHTLY DAMAGED", its efficiency, "CURRENT FUNCTIONALITY: OPERATIVE" or "INOPERATIVE", "ESTIMATED TIME FOR COMPLETE REPAIRS:" in minutes, hours or days, "CURRENTLY UNDER REPAIR.", "NONE" for equipment the ship lacks). The fire button chooses: "NO REPAIRS NEEDED.", "DAMAGED BEYOND REPAIR." at 0, "ALREADY UNDERGOING REPAIRS.", "CEASE REPAIRS ON X AND BEGIN REPAIRS ON Y? [N Y]" with CEASING and CONTINUING REPAIRS ON X, or "BEGINNING REPAIRS ON X."; a finished repair says "REPAIR COMPLETED" (REPAIR-OV) and the engineer waits to be told what to repair next. Following the decision of 2026-10-12 that Repair matches the original, `RepairButton` now does all of this in the original wording, and the engineer then asks for the repair's mineral as before ("cubic meter" for one). Found in the screen comparison (`Research/Screen comparison.md`, section 13). Verified with the new `repairchoice` probe scenario (the cursor, the row texts, a row the ship lacks, beginning, already under repair, No and Yes to ceasing one repair for another, a whole and a destroyed laser), which fails on the old code (8 checks) and passes on the new; `systemrepairs` now chooses the engines and checks that the engineer waits after a repair; `m20`, `m11`, `systemdamage` and `systemeffects` pass, and `screenpictures` captures the cursor.
- **Ledger Uses the Original's Terms**: the original bank ledger has seven transaction types (FINE, SHIP CONFIGURATION, TRADE DEPOT, BONUS, PERSONNEL, INTEREST, GAME PURCHASE: BANKTRANS_STRINGS in the decompiled original). It books the win's 500,000 MU as a BONUS (DPART-OV), a colony evaluation as a BONUS or a FINE (CAPTAIN), and Interstel's supplemental evaluations, the towing charges and the lost terrain vehicle as a FINE (OP-OV). The port wrote "Mission completed", "Colony bonus", "Colony fine", "Towing charges" and "Terrain vehicle replaced"; these are now "Bonus" and "Fine", and "Ship Configuration" becomes "Ship configuration" to match the other rows. "Lifeform data" keeps its name, since the shipped game never pays for recorded lifeforms (they come from the 1984 notes, decision D3). Rows already in a save keep their wording. Found in the screen comparison (`Research/Screen comparison.md`, section 21). Verified with the `colonies`, `distress`, `win` and `lava` probe scenarios, whose ledger checks now expect the original terms (they fail on the old code and pass on the new); `starport-ledger` and `distressconfirm` pass.
- **Bank Pays Interest**: the Bank screen showed INTEREST RATE: 12% and the original ledger has INTEREST rows, but the port never paid any. The original (BANK-OV in the decompiled original, INT%) pays 12% a year on the balance for the days since the last payment, a year being the 300 days of its calendar: the balance times 12 over 100, then times the days over 300, each step rounded down. It pays when the crew boards at the Starport (PORTMENU-OV, after the flight check) and on a new colony recommendation (CAPTAIN); while the interest comes to less than 1 M.U. it pays nothing and the days keep counting. `PlayerData.PayInterest` does the same from the docking bay panel and the recommendation's Yes button, adding an Interest row to the ledger; the day it was paid up to is saved in the bank (`PD_Bank.m_interestDay`), and a save from before this change starts counting the next time, with nothing paid for earlier days. Found in the screen comparison (`Research/Screen comparison.md`, section 21), which now also records that the original books a recommendation's bonus or fine at once, while the port pays it on docking. Verified with the new `starport-interest` probe scenario (the rules, an old save, and the real docking bay: 100,000 M.U. for 150 days earns 6,000), which fails on the old code and passes on the new; `colonies`, `starport-ledger`, `starport-transport`, `starport-win`, `savedata` and `starport-savedata` pass.
- **Probe Landings Choose Their Site in Orbit**: with the landing site now reset on leaving an orbit (#292), the probe helper `LandInTheArthSystemAt` and five scenarios that set the site before entering orbit landed mid-map, failing `ruins`, `artifactsites`, `randomruins`, `lifeforms`, `tvweapons`, `lifebehaviour` and `flyers` in the full run on 9b7e91c8. They now pick the site in orbit, as a player does, and all seven pass. Test tooling only.
- **Landing Site Resets After Leaving Orbit**: Select Site's crosshair started at the last site chosen, on any planet, as only a new game reset it. In the decompiled original, Select Site puts the crosshair at LAT 0 LONG 0 when PLHI is -1 (SITE-OV, WF014), which DEPART sets on leaving a planet's orbit (DPART-OV), and keeps the last site within one orbit. `SpaceflightController.SwitchLocation` now resets the site whenever the ship is in the star system or hyperspace. Found in the screen comparison (`Research/Screen comparison.md`, section 8). The new `landingsite` probe scenario checks the site is kept within an orbit (the control) and reset in the star system and hyperspace (failing on the old code, passing on the new); `m23`, `coordinates`, `tvdisplay` and `landinglines` pass.
- **Original DAMAGE Display**: in the original, Damage clears the auxiliary view and draws a DAMAGE display with no text: a row each for hull, engines, sensors, comm, shield, missiles and laser, each bar green for what is intact and red for what is lost (ENGINEER in the decompiled original: `(/DAMAGE)`, `DBARS`; Repair opens with it too). The port wrote a "Damage Report:" text. `DamageDisplay`, built at run time by `DisplayController` with the UI kit's styles (the scene has no object for it), now draws it: Damage shows it without text, Repair before the engineer speaks, and its bars follow repairs in progress. Shield charge stays on the Status gauge. Repair still takes the worst system first (decided 2026-10-11); letting the player choose, as the original does, remains an open decision. Found in the screen comparison (`Research/Screen comparison.md`, section 13). Verified with the `m11` and `systemdamage` probe scenarios, whose old report checks now read the bars (failing on the old code, which has no display, and passing on the new), and a picture of the display in `screenpictures`.
- **Weather Shown Alone on the Surface**: in the original, the first line on a planet's surface is the weather, alone in the text window ("IT IS CLEAR.", starflt_179); the port wrote it under the five disembarking lines. `PlanetWeather.Begin` now clears the text window before the first sky. Found in the screen comparison (`Research/Screen comparison.md`, section 10). A new `disembarklines` check sees six lines on the old code and the weather alone on the new; `storms`, `look` and `tvdisplay` pass.
- **Screen Comparison Brought Up to Date**: `Research/Screen comparison.md` now counts 43 of 52 game logic items fixed, 5 open and 4 not a defect or a design decision, names the merges since the second check (#281 to #301) and lists what remains: booking the colony bonus or fine at once (a design decision), MOTION DETECTED AT X: Y: (parked: the original's numbers are alien groups' hourly steps, which the port does not model) and three low-priority rows, plus the 2026-10-12 decision to keep the UI kit's own wording. Documentation only.
- **Screen Comparison With the Original**: `Research/Screen comparison.md` records every difference between the port's screens and the original's (roadmap Phase 7, screens half), rechecked on 2026-10-12 against the probe's new pictures of the flight screens and Starport rooms, and the decompiled original where screenshots run out. A table per screen gives each item's status (fixed and by which PR, open, partly, not a defect, a design decision, or a gap in the probe's pictures), area (game logic or UI kit) and evidence. Of 51 game logic items, 22 are fixed and 25 open, including the DAMAGE display, Repair's choice of system, bank interest (the screen says 12% and nothing pays it), the notices screen, combat messages and wounds, and MOTION DETECTED AT X: Y:; its Step 2 list orders them. The 40 open UI kit items are only recorded. Documentation only.
- **Message Audit Against STRINFO**: `Research/STRINFO message audit.md` lists every player-visible message that differs from STRINFO (roadmap Phase 7): comm lines, notices, evaluations, Starport analyses and prices, ruin messages, story texts and texts in C#, each with the port's text, STRINFO's (section and line), file and line, and a category. Where they disagree it adds the shipped game's text from the decompiled original (`s-macke/starflight-reverse`: `instance.txt`, `data.h`, the overlays). The game data's comm lines are the shipped game's: in all 47 where STRINFO differs, the shipped game agrees with the game data. Its notices and analyses follow STRINFO where it departs from the shipped game, by design decision. The bugs found (a ruin sign showing a literal "[NUMBER]", a Thrynn offer printing "unitxx") are left to follow-up PRs per area; the game is unchanged. `ROADMAP.md` links it from Phase 7. Its step 2 list records what later fixes covered (#239, #243 and #246) and what remains; the Noah 9 beacon's lines proved to be the shipped game's own phrases for the derelict, as the port has them.
- **Colony Evaluations Get Original Headings and Planet Names**: Interstel's colony evaluations said only "GOOD WORK! ..." or "THIS PLANET PROVED UNSUITABLE ...", so several at one docking were indistinguishable. The original records each recommendation and heads each evaluation (OP-OV in the decompiled original) with "RECOMMENDATION: n" (counted over the game), "DATE:", "SYSTEM: x,y", "ORBIT:", "NAME:" and "EVALUATION:". The name is the player's: after Yes to "Recommend this planet for colonization?" the original asks "NAME THIS PLANET: " (up to 15 characters), then says "RECOMMENDATION LOGGED." (CAPTAIN overlay, `(/LOG-PLAN)`). The port now does both: `RecommendYesButton` reads the name on the console until Enter (Backspace deletes, nothing is typed while the save panel is open, and without a keyboard the planet stays unnamed) and says "Recommendation logged." instead of "Recommendation noted."; `PD_General.ColonyRecommendation` saves number, date and name (older saves have none, so their pending recommendations get only SYSTEM and ORBIT); and `PlayerData.EvaluateColonyRecommendations` adds the headings. Found by the message audit (`Research/STRINFO message audit.md`, section 3). The `colonies` probe scenario's three new checks (the question and "Recommendation logged.", Mardan 4's heading with NAME: NEW EDEN, a name cut to 15 characters and an empty one) fail on the old code and pass on the new; its four other checks, `savedata`, `starport-savedata`, `h7` and `starport-win` pass.
- **Veloxi Drones Grant Permission to Orbit**: the five Veloxi drone encounters guard their planets from orbit, and since in-orbit encounters arrived they blocked all landings there for good. As in the original (STRINFO 2.3), a drone with the comm link up now asks the player to confirm three numbers from 1 to 99, about half of them multiples of 6 (the sources give no count; three was chosen). Yes to each multiple of 6 and no to the rest earns "Permission to orbit granted": the ship returns to orbit and can land, unchallenged until it leaves the star system, and is asked again on a later visit (by design; nothing new is saved). A wrong answer, or ending the link before the last number, gets "Permission to orbit denied", and the drone cuts the link and attacks. These two lines are the first records of the new `Assets/Resources/Starflight Recovered Data.json`, which holds what the original had and `Starflight Game Data.json` lacks, recovered from STRINFO (ids from 1000, each with its source); `DataController.LoadGameData` merges it in (`GameData.AddRecoveredData`), so `Starflight Game Data.json` stays the original data. `GD_Comm.Subject` gains `OrbitGranted` and `OrbitDenied`. Verified with the `drones` probe scenario.
- **Veloxi Ship Models**: the Veloxi Transport, Scout, Warship and Drone (vessels 11, 12, 13 and 18) were clones of the "Not Modeled Yet" placeholder, a stretched sphere with the UV grid test material, in encounters and in orbit (five of the twelve in-orbit encounters are Veloxi drones). They now have their own models and debris: procedural low-poly stand-ins, not hand-made art, after the original sensor pictures (`Research/Screenshots/Ships`): sensor boom, crescent wing and red engine pods (two pairs on the warship), cargo lobes on the transport, and a needle-shaped drone with two red cores. Sizes follow STRINFO 7.2 against the player's ship (51 units): transport and warship 1, scout 0.6, drone 0.3. The new `DevTools/ShipModels` scripts (Blender 5.0) build them: `ship_kit.py` (shapes and the size rule chosen on 2026-10-09), a `build_<race>.py` per race, review sheets in `previews/`, and `wire.ps1`, which headlessly makes the materials (SF - Standard, flat colours), sets up imports and points the scene's template slots at the models. The `shipmodels` probe scenario now checks each new vessel's length, that it is longest nose to tail with engines aft, and its debris.
- **Thrynn Ship Models**: the Thrynn Transport, Scout and Warship (vessels 8, 9 and 10) replace the placeholder with models and debris: procedural stand-ins from `DevTools/ShipModels/build_thrynn.py` after the original sensor pictures, an oval or round grey-green hull with a window band or light in front and red engine pods (four on the warship, plus laser cannons and sensor spikes). Sizes against the player's ship: warship 0.8, scout 0.4 (STRINFO 7.4); STRINFO omits the transport, so its 600 tons give 1.2 by the rule the other entries follow (mass over 500 tons). Verified with the `shipmodels` probe scenario.
- **Elowan Ship Models**: the Elowan Transport, Scout and Warship (vessels 5, 6 and 7) replace the placeholder with models and debris: procedural stand-ins from `DevTools/ShipModels/build_elowan.py` after the original sensor pictures. The plant-race Elowan fly asymmetric ships: a green seed pod or spindle, forward blades with striped cyan light panels, and red engine pods placed differently on each side (the warship is two slender hulls joined at the back by a curved claw). Sizes against the player's ship (STRINFO 7.3): transport 0.7, warship 0.3, scout 0.1 shown at the 0.3 floor. Verified with the `shipmodels` probe scenario.
- **Uhlek Ship Models**: the Uhlek Scout and Warship (vessels 16 and 17) replace the placeholder with models and debris: procedural stand-ins from `DevTools/ShipModels/build_uhlek.py` after the original sensor pictures, a dark purple ring open at the front around a blue-lit core with a glowing red heart at the back, and a second inner ring on the warship. Sizes against the player's ship (STRINFO 7.12): scout 2; the warship's 10 is shown at 5.0 by the size rule for the biggest vessels. Verified with the `shipmodels` probe scenario.
- **Gazurtoid Ship Models**: the Gazurtoid Scout and Warship (vessels 14 and 15) replace the placeholder with models and debris: procedural stand-ins from `DevTools/ShipModels/build_gazurtoid.py` after the original sensor pictures, a ragged, dark organic mass with spines, scattered cyan lights and a magenta-red core (lumps and spines from a fixed seed). STRINFO 7.5 calls them huge, 60 and 90 times the player's ship; the size rule for the biggest vessels shows them at 7.0 and 7.5 times (358 and 381 units). Verified with the `shipmodels` probe scenario.
- **Noah 9 Derelict and Enterprise Ship Models**: the last two placeholder vessels, the Noah 9 derelict transport (vessel 23, met in orbit) and the Enterprise (vessel 21), get models with debris: procedural stand-ins from `DevTools/ShipModels/build_noah.py` and `build_enterprise.py` after the original sensor pictures. The derelict, 4 times the player's ship, is a pointed nose on a ragged block of scorched cargo modules with nothing that glows (STRINFO 7.10 gives it no energy). The Enterprise, the 1986 original's easter egg, is recognizable on purpose (chosen 2026-10-09): saucer, neck, secondary hull with deflector, and two red-capped nacelles; STRINFO 7.13's 38 times is shown at 6.5 by the size rule. No game data vessel clones the placeholder any more. Verified with the `shipmodels` probe scenario.
- **Lifeform Models**: surface lifeforms have review models, not yet in the game: one per body plan of the 1984 design notes (snakelike; bilateral with no, one, two, four or many legs; radial with no, few or many legs; amorphous with and without legs) and two per plant size (small, medium, large; types A and B), 17 in all, in `Assets/Game Objects/Lifeforms/`. The new `DevTools/LifeformModels` scripts (Blender 5.2) build organic bodies from signed distance fields (smooth unions of spheres, ellipsoids and round cones, polygonized by surface nets) and bake skin relief and painted patterns in Cycles into albedo, specular and smoothness, normal and occlusion maps (plus emission for glowing eyes and pods) on 1,300 to 4,000 triangle meshes. Each fits a 1 unit cube, faces Unity +Z, and has a `Skin` material for the species tint and an untinted `Detail` material (eyes, claws, wood, pods). Review renders are in `DevTools/LifeformModels/previews/`.
- **More Original Content Recovered From STRINFO**: `Assets/Resources/Starflight Recovered Data.json` gains four lists from `Research/Data/STRINFO.DOC` that the game data lacked. Planet messages (3.1): 38 texts at 35 places on 22 planets, at ruin coordinates or random places, such as Earth's newspapers. Artifact sites (4.1): 14 sites of the 12 special artifacts on planets, three for the Black Egg. Colony evaluations (1.2): the 51 planets rated Optimal or Suitable that earn a Starport bonus, 30,000 to 55,000 MU. Story texts: ten, for the evaluations, the win, the flare and the distress call. No gameplay uses them yet; the ruins, special artifacts, colony recommendations and endgame (roadmap phases 1, 3 and 5) will. Texts are as STRINFO prints them, in capitals, with brackets for values the game fills in. Planets are named as in STRINFO, by star coordinates and number from the sun, and `GameData.Initialize` resolves every record (`GameData.FindPlanetFromSun`). `m_note` records one correction and two conflicts. STRINFO prints the Hypercube's star two units off the planet it names, where a message lies at the same coordinates, so the named planet is used. The Rod Device and Koann 3 stay as printed pending roadmap decision D5: STRINFO puts the Rod Device at Harrison's first base in its artifact list but his second in New Scotland's entry, and prints Koann 3 as planet 4 while its name, orbit number and an Elowan message say 3. Verified with the `recovereddata` probe scenario, which also shows the orbital guards waiting at the planets STRINFO names for the Black Egg, the Crystal Orb, Heaven and the Shimmering Ball.
- **Ruins Carry the Original Messages**: planets had no ruins; `TerrainRuins` was in no scene and would have scattered random ruins with random artifacts. It now places a ruin wherever the original has a message (recovered from STRINFO 3.1), at the message's latitude and longitude and shared by messages at one site (one site on Earth holds an invoice and the 3480 flare log, one on Mardan 2 three texts). A message STRINFO places at "random locations" or "several locations" gets its own ruin at a spot drawn from the planet's seed, the same every visit (decided 2026-10-09). Earth has 12 ruins. Each is one of the five Ancient Ruins models, picked by its message, with nearby rocks and trees cleared. The ruins' random draws come last, so no rock, deposit or tree moves. Beside a ruin, the terrain vehicle's Cargo button logs its messages once each under Messages in the ship's log, dated when found ("any messages you find are identified by the date found", manual page 21; `PD_ShipsLog.AddFoundMessage`). Scan labels and counts the ruins. `Spaceflight.unity` gains a "Terrain Ruins" object with the five models under the terrain vehicle's grid. Random ruins from the 1984 design notes are not placed (roadmap 3.5). Verified with the `ruins` probe scenario.
- **Special Artifacts at Their Sites**: the 12 special artifacts hidden on planets (14 sites from STRINFO 4.1, three for the Black Egg) now lie in ruins at their latitude and longitude. A site shared with a message has one ruin, as two of the artifacts' sites are. Other sites get their own ruin, placed after the message ruins without random numbers, so no ruin, rock, deposit or tree moves. Beside such a ruin, the terrain vehicle's Cargo button takes the artifact into the vehicle's hold if there is room (saying so if not), while recording the ruin's messages. It can be taken once: `PD_PlanetSurfaces` saves the taken sites. Back in the ship, it moves to the ship's hold. Scan labels a ruin still holding an artifact "Ruin - artifact" and counts the artifacts, the label serving as marker until there are models. Two STRINFO conflicts were settled on 2026-10-09 (roadmap decision D5): the Rod Device lies at Harrison's base 2, as New Scotland's own entry says, sharing the ruin of the report that found that base; and the Red Cylinder's planet is the third from its sun, as its name says, for the cylinder and the planet's colony evaluation. Verified with the `artifactsites` probe scenario.
- **Most Magnificent Hexagon and City of the Ancients**: STRINFO 4.1 puts the Crystal Orb "in the center of a circular formation of 6 ancient ruins" and the Crystal Pearl "in the southwest of a cluster of 15 ancient ruins". Both formations now surround their artifacts' ruins: the Hexagon's six in a circle 60 degrees apart, the City's fifteen in rows of four north and east of the Pearl's ruin, which takes the block's southwest corner. They are recovered data records (`m_ruinFormationList`, `GD_RuinFormation`), placed last on the planet without random numbers, and hold nothing. STRINFO gives counts and shapes but no sizes, so the Hexagon's ruins sit 48 units from the Orb's and the City's 40 units apart, noted in each record. Verified with the `formations` probe scenario.
- **Dropping Cargo From the Terrain Vehicle**: the manual (page 21) has the Cargo option list the terrain vehicle's load "and give you the option of dropping anything", adding that "when an object is dropped on a planet's surface it can be picked up again". With nothing in reach, the Cargo button now lists the hold (elements, then artifacts) with the first item marked, and the console shows Next, Drop and Back. Next marks the next item, Drop sets all of the marked item down at the vehicle, and Back, like dropping the last item, returns to the vehicle's buttons. A dropped item is saved with its planet and position (`PD_PlanetSurfaces.m_droppedCargoList`), stays there on every visit, shows as "Dropped cargo" in Scan, and the Cargo button beside it takes back as much as the hold has room for. It lies at the vehicle's height, since the vehicle floats on water above lower ground. The selection method and the small stand-in ruin model are the port's choices; the sources describe neither. Verified with the `dropcargo` probe scenario.
- **Terrain Vehicle Cargo Display**: `TerrainVehicleCargoDisplay` was in no scene. It is now the console's "Cargo" display while the terrain vehicle's cargo list is open (the second half of roadmap 3.4, decided 2026-10-09), listing the hold's elements and artifacts with volumes and how full the hold is; Back or dropping the last item restores the terrain vehicle display. It rebuilds its text only when the hold changes, not every frame. In `Spaceflight.unity` it is a copy of the terrain vehicle display's panel with its first block's labels and values, as tall as both blocks, registered as `DisplayController.m_terrainVehicleCargoDisplay`; the scene only gains objects. The display is not in the sources, and its look has not been checked by eye. Verified with the `cargodisplay` probe scenario.
- **Crystal Planet Field and the Crystal Orb**: STRINFO 5.1 says the Crystal Planet "generates a powerful magnetic force which can only be negated by the Crystal Orb" and that ships orbiting without it "will take on damage rapidly"; the original kernel has a "crystal planet heating routine". The port had none of this. A ship in its orbit without the Orb in the hold now takes 5 points of damage a second, shields then armor like all damage, but none while climbing from or descending to the surface; with the Orb aboard the field does nothing. The rate is the port's choice, as the sources say only "rapidly". The planet is known by its game data type (`GD_Planet.IsCrystalPlanet`). This covers roadmap item 5.2 and the Orb's effect from Phase 4. Verified with the `crystalfield` probe scenario.
- **Black Egg Can Destroy a Planet**: the Starport's analysis says "to activate it, you must drop it", the original kernel stores the planet of an egg "dropped and armed" (`?BOMB`), and the original game's screens show the rest: back in orbit, "Captain, we are receiving a countdown transmission from the Black Egg...", 5 to 1, "Boom!", and the planet is gone. The port had none of this. A Black Egg dropped from the terrain vehicle's cargo list is now armed (the messages say so), and once the ship is back above its star system's planets it counts down a number a second and destroys the planet: saved as destroyed (`PD_PlanetSurfaces.m_destroyedPlanetList`), it vanishes from the system, its system display and the Sensors count, and a ship orbiting it is left in the star system. The Crystal Planet falls only to an egg at its control nexus, with Interstel's message (STRINFO 2.12); elsewhere on it, "Crystal planet damaged but not destroyed" and the egg is spent. Destroying Elan or the Uhlek mind-ganglion's planet brings the crew's report from STRINFO 5.1. The countdown text and both reports join the recovered story texts. Picking an egg up again before the ship is back up disarms it. The port's choices: a second per number, how near the nexus the egg must lie (1 degree each way), and the countdown also running in the star system (an encounter beginning in orbit would otherwise stop it). Not done here: the win itself (500,000 MU, the medal, the end of flares, roadmap 5.4) and what losing the mind-ganglion or Elan does to those races (5.3). Verified with the `blackegg` probe scenario.
- **Crystal Cone Locates the Control Nexus**: the research council's recommendation found on Arth (STRINFO 3.1) says the Crystal Cone "is needed to identify from orbit the location of the control nexus" of the Crystal Planet, the one place a Black Egg destroys it, and the Elowan agree ("firstly must thou find the nexus of control"). The port gave the Cone no use. With it in the hold, the messages now report that the Crystal Cone has located the control nexus, with its latitude and longitude, on entering the Crystal Planet's orbit (after any launch from its surface) and each time Land opens the site map there; without it, or at any other planet, nothing is said. The sources do not say how the original showed this, so the messages are the port's choice (`CrystalCone.ReportNexus`). Verified with the `crystalcone` probe scenario.
